Tool result:
## 3. What separates strong from loose (the empirical comparison)

I picked ten explanations that were clearly strong and ten that were complete but loose, read all twenty side by side, then measured a few things.

- **Strong (10):** PT139-S4-Q18 Flaw, PT142-S4-Q22 Flaw, PT145-S2-Q19 Paradox, PT154-S1-Q18 NA, PT153-S2-Q13 Weaken, PT146-S2-Q13 Strengthen, PT147-S1-Q17 Evaluate, PT152-S2-Q21 Principle-Conform, PT150-S3-Q15 Agree/Disagree, PT144-S3-Q17 Role.
- **Loose but complete (10), with the failure each shows:** PT127-S2-Q3 Weaken (two wrong answers get one line each: "This supports the argument's explanation"); PT126-S4-Q20 Weaken (generic summary notes, a Goal that restates the task); PT124-S3-Q10 Role (short, and "Haha. This answer is unthinkably weird"); PT151-S2-Q18 Strengthen (117 words total; the 29% trap gets one sentence); PT146-S2-Q8 Flaw (every wrong answer is "The argument never discusses X", correct note is one line); PT142-S4-Q5 Principle (mechanical, no reason why the trap tempts); PT155-S2-Q10 Paradox (fine idea, thin notes); PT158-S3-Q2 Strengthen (long, but misquotes a choice); PT158-S2-Q16 Paradox (long, but brings in outside facts); PT148-S1-Q20 Method (sound analysis, but calls the correct answer "so, so dumb").

Measured averages (strong vs loose):

| Measure | Strong | Loose |
|---|---|---|
| Total words | 773 | 394 |
| Words in the Evaluate step | 169 | 77 |
| Words in the Goal step | 66 | 43 |
| Goal names 3+ content words from the stimulus | 0.9 | 0.5 |
| Avg words per wrong-answer note | 87 | 45 |
| Wrong-answer notes under 20 words (verdict only) | 0.0 per question | 1.1 per question |
| Wrong-answer notes that run an explicit test (negate it, does it weaken, does it trigger the rule, why should we believe it) | 1.4 of 4 | 0.2 of 4 |
| Words in the correct-answer note | 143 | 60 |
| Examples, analogies or worked numbers | 2.0 | 0.5 |
| Stated generalizations ("whenever", "on X questions", "usually") | 1.6 | 0.3 |

What actually separates them, in order of how reliably it predicted my judgment:

1. **The Evaluate step argues with the argument.** Strong ones do real work before the choices: they build the objection ("Given that X, how could we argue not-Y?"), do the arithmetic (25% of $400 vs 13% of $1,000), or set the two populations side by side. Loose ones restate the stimulus and move on.
2. **The Goal names the answer, not the task.** Strong: "look for an answer saying the fish in the leafless trees are exposed to predators that hunt by sight." Loose: "Find the flaw that mistakes a response to danger for a cause", "Identify an answer choice that supports fairness." Strong Goals often give two acceptable shapes (a linking answer or a defender).
3. **Every wrong answer gets a reason tied to this question, and usually a test.** The loose group's signature is the verdict-only note: "This is irrelevant.", "That is unnecessary.", "The argument never discusses profits." Strong notes say what the answer claims, what was needed, and where they diverge, often by running the family's test on it (negate it; would it weaken; is the trigger established; does the conclusion match the rule's right side).
4. **The popular trap gets the most attention.** Strong explanations spend their longest wrong-answer note on the choice that drew 15-30% of students and say why it tempts. Loose ones treat it like every other wrong answer.
5. **The correct-answer note explains the fit, and often generalizes it.** Strong: "Another correct answer performing the same function could have said..." (PT146-S2-Q13); the karaoke/Jenga reason why A → C still works when you predicted A and B → C (PT139-S4-Q21). Loose: "This is the flaw."
6. **Fidelity.** Two loose items are long. Length did not save them: a misquoted answer choice, imported outside facts, or the author disowning the question destroys model value regardless of word count.

What did not separate them: word count by itself (PT152-S4-Q19 is 479 words and strong; PT158-S2-Q16 is 716 and loose), the choice-tag vocabulary, and the presence of jokes (several strong ones joke; it only hurts when the joke is at the question's or the answer's expense).

## 4. Rubric used for the shortlist

Gate (hard requirement): full Argument breakdown and all five choice notes. Every shortlisted candidate passed.

Scored 0 / 1 / 2:

| Criterion | 2 | 1 | 0 |
|---|---|---|---|
| **P** Prediction | Goal names the content or shape of the right answer before the choices (may give two acceptable shapes) | Goal names the task or admits it cannot predict | No prediction |
| **X** Precision | Names the exact gap, flaw or relationship in this argument's words | Uses the right label but stays generic | Label only, or wrong |
| **E** Eliminations | Every wrong answer gets a question-grounded reason; the popular trap gets the most attention | Most do; one or two verdict-only notes | Verdict-only notes |
| **T** Transfer | States the move in a form that works on the next question of this type (the test, the rule, the recurring trap) | Transfer is implicit | None |
| **C** Clarity | Plain, no padding, no pasted repetition | Some padding or pasted notes | Hard to follow |
| **L** Learner value | Teaches what a student needs to understand and repeat the move: why the trap tempts, what to do when stuck, a worked example or analogy, a second correct answer that would also work | Justifies the key well but does not teach the move | Only justifies the key |

Plus a **fidelity flag** (not scored; disqualifying if serious): `slip` factual or typo error, `tone` the author mocks the question or answer, `register` explicit-Standard PT157/158 prose, `transcription` damaged text.

Learner value is the criterion that most often separated a 10 from a 12. Many explanations justify the key correctly (1) but only the best ones also show the student the move they will reuse (2).

## 6. Recurring phrasing conventions

- **Frames:** "Given that ... how could we (still) argue that ..." (Weaken, Strengthen, NA, Flaw); "Curious Fact ... Author's Explanation ... Alternate Explanation / Plausibility" (any causal argument); "Given that ... How can it be that ..." (Paradox); "If Evidence, then Conclusion" (Principle, SA).
- **Tests in choice notes:** negate it and ask if it weakens (NA); "fails to consider X" means ask whether X weakens, "presumes X" means ask whether X is necessary (Flaw); "argues X by Y: X must match the conclusion and Y the evidence" (Method, Flaw); a rule can only prove its right-hand side (Principle, SA); "can we support the Agree position and the Disagree position?" (Agree/Disagree); "Why should we believe this?" (Role, Main Conclusion).
- **Recurring trap names:** Too Strong (the most common NA and MBT tag), Out of Scope, No Impact, Unclear Impact, No Distinction (Paradox), Deepens Paradox, Wrong Flaw / Not an Objection (Flaw), Bad Conclusion / Evidence / Trigger Match (Principle, Parallel), Premise / Last-Claim Trap / Opposing Position (Main Conclusion), Unsupported Agree/Disagree Position (Agree/Disagree), Fake Opposite / Illegal Negation (NA), Reversal (SA). Tags come from the local markdown; the dump does not include them.
- **Rules stated as rules:** "Most X can only be concluded from a Most X premise"; "some / sometimes / can is almost always wrong on Strengthen, Weaken, Paradox, SA"; "the second speaker almost never denies the first speaker's evidence"; "about 85-90% of Main Conclusion questions put the conclusion in the first sentence or after a pivot"; "on EXCEPT, three of the working answers usually work one way and one works another".
- **Voice habits that should not be copied:** mocking the question or answer ("hot garbage", "so, so dumb", "unthinkably weird", "dumpster fire"), and the PT157/158 em-dash register.

## What separates a top-tier explanation from a loose one

Length does not. These do:

1. **The Evaluate step argues with the argument** ("How could we argue the opposite?") instead of restating it.
2. **The Goal names the answer** it expects, not just the task.
3. **Every wrong answer gets a reason and usually a test.** Loose ones average one verdict-only note per question ("This is irrelevant").
4. **The most popular trap gets the most attention.**
5. **The correct-answer note teaches the move**, so a student can repeat it on the next question, not just confirm the key.

Complete (full breakdown plus all five Choice Notes) is a gate, not a score.

## Do not copy from the corpus

Joshua, 2026-09-26: a personal jab at the test is fine; it makes the voice personal.


- The PT157/158 explicit-Standard register: heavy em-dashes and formulaic phrasing. None of those made the set.
- Typos and slips carried in the source (flagged in the table).

## Where the set is thin

- **Refute a Conditional**: 14 gap questions, no strong exemplar anywhere in the corpus. The guide for it will lean on the conditional picks (#2, #9, #35, #44) and LSAT Hacks for context.
- **Paradox**: hard and EXCEPT now covered by #51 (PT106-S2-Q26).
- **Method on Plans / Recommendations** (15 gap questions) has no pick.
- Most other empty cells are topic features (Science, Medical) that do not change how an explanation is built, so they do not need their own model.

=================== PT148-S3-Q3 | diff 1 | key B | score 24.9 | features: Explain Curious Fact, Plausibility
STIMULUS: Researcher: Dinosaur fossils come in various forms, including mineralized bones and tracks in dried mud flats. However, mineralized dinosaur bones and dinosaur tracks in dried mud flats are rarely found together. This isn’t surprising, because creatures that scavenged dinosaur carcasses most likely frequented mud flats to find food.
STEM: Which one of the following, if true, would most strengthen the researcher’s argument?
(A) [2% | Out of Scope: other locations] Dinosaur tracks are also found in locations other than mud flats.
(B) [91% | Correct] Scavengers commonly drag a carcass away from the site where it was found.
(C) [1% | Irrelevant Comparison] Researchers have found more fossil dinosaur tracks than fossil dinosaur bones.
(D) [2% | Out of Scope: other types of fossils] Dinosaur fossils other than mineralized bone or tracks in dried mud flats are quite common.
(E) [4% | Irrelevant Comparison] It takes longer for bone to mineralize than it takes for tracks to dry in mud flats.
--- BREAKDOWN
## Conclusion
It's not surprising that we usually don't find mineralized dinosaur bones and dinosaur mud tracks in the same area.
## Evidence
Creatures that scavenged dinosaur carcasses most likely went to mud flats to find food.
## Evaluate
This is one of those arguments that is super hard to understand on a first (or second) read, but if we can figure out the author's intended meaning, then we're actually 90% of the way to our correct answer.
  There are two types of dinosaur fossils, but we don't find them in the same place usually. We either find bones or find footprints in the mud.
  The author has a theory for why we don't find them together, and since our author is trying to explain a puzzling phenomenon, we can use our Curious Fact / Explanation mindset:
  Curious Fact ... why don't we find bones and mud tracks in the same place?
  Author's Explanation ... animals that would scavenge dinosaur carcasses frequented mud flats to find food.
  Again, what?! We need to make sense of causal story the author is telling here. It might help to think about what it would be like if we did find dinosaur bones and mud tracks in the same place:
  It would mean that a dinosaur died in the mud flat. We're generally finding dinosaur bones at the spot where they died (they're too heavy and not-useful for animals to have moved them, so other than weather events like a hurricane or earthquake moving them, they will be found where the dinosaur died).
  So if a dinosaur died in a mud flat, it would definitely leave some footprints in the mud en route to its death scene, right? How come we don't see those final footprints in the mud?
  The author is saying that there were scavenger animals who roamed mud flats looking for food. If they happened upon a dead dinosaur, then they would go to town on its carcass.
  So the author is either thinking that we don't find bones in the mud flats because there were scavengers roaming the mud flats who would perform "clean up duty" and eat the bones.
  Or ... the author is thinking that we don't find muddy dinosaur footprints near the dinosaurs bones, because the footprints of the scavengers that were eating the dinosaurs carcass messed up the dinosaurs mud footprint (kind of like if someone walks through snow, they leave footprints, but if you walk through their footprints, you can't clearly see that person's boot print anymore).
## Goal
Since this is Strengthen, we are most likely adding plausibility to the author's story (i.e. scavengers in the mud flats would have consumed the dinosaur's bones or obscured the dinosaur's footprints), or we might be ruling out an alternate explanation for why bones and mud prints aren't found in the same place.
--- CHOICE NOTES
(A) This argument is only about why we don't find bones near mud flat tracks. Talking about other areas isn't going to help us assess this causal mystery.
(B) This doesn't give either of the two scavenger-stories I was considering (scavengers eat the bones / scavengers ruin the fidelity of the dinosaur's muddy footprints), but it could still help connect scavengers in the mud to why we don't find dinosaur bones near dinosaur mud tracks.

If scavengers were dragging a dinosaur carcass away from the mud flats, then that would help explain why we don't find dinosaur bones in the mud flats where we find dinosaur tracks.

This answer is helping us picture this chronological story:
1. Dinosaur walks into the mud flats, leaving behind tracks.
2. Dinosaur dies in the mud flats.
3. Scavenger drags the carcass away, so therefore we usually don't find dinosaur bones near tracks in the mud.
(C) It doesn't matter in the slightest which of these two things we've found more of. We're just trying to solve the causal mystery of why we don't seem to find these two things in the same place.
(D) The fact that other types of fossils are common was kind of covered in the opening clause, "Dinosaur fossils come in various forms".

We're focused on only two forms — mineralized bone / tracks in dried mud. And we're trying to solve the causal mystery of why we don't usually find dinosaur bones near dinosaur tracks in dried mud.

Pointing out that other types of dinosaur fossils exist does nothing to help us assess this causal mystery.
(E) This is similar to (C), in that it provides a difference between bones and mud tracks that is completely useless when it comes to solving our causal mystery.

It doesn't matter to us that it took longer for the bone to become mineralized than for the muddy footprints to dry. They've both had ample time (over 65 million years) to do their thang. They're both fossils now. We're just trying to figure out why we don't find them near each other.

126	=================== PT128-S3-Q1 | diff 1 | key C | score 23.3 | features: Plausibility
127	STIMULUS: In a transportation company, a certain syndrome often attributed to stress by medical experts afflicts a significantly higher percentage of workers in Department F than in any other department. We can conclude, therefore, that the work done in Department F subjects workers to higher stress levels than does the work in the other departments in the company.
128	STEM: Which one of the following, if true, most helps to support the argument?
129	(A) [2% | No Impact] Department F has more employees than any other department in the company.
130	(B) [2% | Weakens, if anything] Some experts believe that the syndrome can be caused by various factors, only one of which is high stress.
131	(C) [94% | Correct] Many workers who transfer into Department F from elsewhere in the company soon begin to develop the syndrome.
132	(D) [1% | No Impact] It is relatively common for workers in the transportation industry to suffer from the syndrome.
133	(E) [1% | Unclear Impact] Job-related stress has been the most frequently cited cause for dissatisfaction among workers at the company.
134	--- BREAKDOWN
135	## Conclusion (conclude, therefore)
136	The work done in Department F subjects workers to higher stress levels than does the work in the other departments in the company.j
137	## Evidence
138	Department F has a higher percentage of workers with syndrome X than does any other department, and syndrome X is often attributed to stress.
139	## Evaluate
140	This argument follows a common pattern: - it presents a Curious Fact (almost always an asymmetric comparison) in the evidence - the conclusion assumes or states a causal explanation for that curious fact. Curious Fact ... why do workers in Dept F have a higher rate of getting this syndrome that's associated with stress? Author's Explanation ... it must be that workers in Dept F undergo more stress than do workers in other departments. We always approach Explain Curious Fact arguments looking at the same two pressure points: - Alternate Explanations for the curious fact - the plausibility of the Author's Explanation What could be an alternate reason for why people in Dept F have higher rates of this syndrome? - maybe the syndrome isn't really caused by stress; it's caused by something else like lack of sleep, and people in Dept F get less sleep. - maybe it is caused by stress, but the stress isn't coming from Dept F; it's just that the workers who work in Dept F have more stressful lives for other reasons (maybe Dept F is full of parents of young children, whereas the other departments more often have people without children or with low-maintenance children).
141	## Goal
142	Since this is Strengthen, we would want an answer to rule out an Alternate Explanation. Or, more commonly on Strengthen, we would want it to increase the plausibility of the Author's Explanation. The most common way to increase plausibility of a Cause / Effect connection on LSAT is to hear about data points that show No Cause / No Effect (i.e. when the cause was absent, the effect was absent). So if we heard something like "once people leave Department F, the syndrome goes away", that would heavily suggest that Department F was the causal difference-maker responsible for the syndrome.
143	--- CHOICE NOTES
144	(A) The raw numbers of employees doesn't make any difference, since our evidence is about percentage of workers.
145	Some people might interpret this answer as boosting the plausibility of the claim that Department F subjects workers to higher stress levels. After all, they might think, "the more people there are in your department, the more stressed you'll be".
146	That is not a common sense connection. Sure, for introverts having more people around could increase stress. But it's also possible that departments that are short-staffed are more stressed because there's too much work being spread among too few employees (meanwhile having lots of employees could mean that in Department F, each person's workload is smaller and thus less stressful).
147	(B) The weak language of "some" is almost always wrong on Strengthen, Weaken, and Paradox.
148	In this case, this answer is suggesting that the syndrome might be caused by something other than stress, which would weaken the argument by creating the potential for an Alternate Explanation for why Dept F suffers from a higher rate of this syndrome.
149	(C) This strengthens the plausibility of the Author's Explanation.
150	She thought that the higher stress level of Department F explained why more people there get this certain syndrome.
151	This answer corroborates that idea by saying that many people who transfer from elsewhere in the company into Department F soon begin to develop the syndrome.
152	This is the sort of covariation strengthener that's so common.
153	When they weren't in Department F (when they worked elsewhere in this company), they didn't have the syndrome. That shows No Cause, No Effect.
154	When they transfer into Department F, they develop the syndrome.
155	This answer doesn't prove the conclusion, naturally. No correct answer to Strengthen does that. In fact this answer doesn't directly address anything about stress levels in Department F. But this answer makes it seem pretty clear that the cause of the syndrome is directly connected to being in Department F.
156	It somewhat rules out the idea of alternate explanations related to the specific employees, because these employees didn't have the syndrome when they worked elsewhere in the company. Coming to Department F seems to have caused the syndrome. And since the syndrome is commonly thought to be caused by stress, it heavily suggests that Department F is extra-stressful.
157	(D) We don't care in any absolute or broad sense how common or rare this syndrome is among transportation industry workers.
158	We only care about solving the causal mystery behind why this syndrome afflicts a higher percentage of workers in Department F vs. other departments at this company.
159	Lupus is a fairly uncommon disease, but if one elementary school had a higher rate of lupus (say, 2% of students had lupus) than did other elementary schools (where only 0.3% of students had lupus), we would assume that something weird is going on with that school that has the higher rate of lupus.
160	(E) This answer is somewhat tempting because it helps to establish the plausibility of the idea that working at this company is stressful.
161	After all, if stress is the most common reason that workers there say they're dissatisfied, then it's reasonable to think that working there is stressful.
162	But this doesn't do anything to help us support the claim that Department F is special when it comes to stress.
163	If the conclusion were merely, "the occurrence of this syndrome is being caused by stress", then this would do a better job of strengthening. But since the conclusion is specifically about Department F having more stress than other departments, this answer isn't doing anything to make that claim more plausible.

167	STIMULUS: After a hepadnavirus inserts itself into a chromosome of an animal, fragments of the virus are passed on to all of that animal’s descendants. A hepadnavirus fragment is present in a chromosome of the zebra finch and in precisely the same location in a corresponding chromosome of the dark-eyed junco. The fact that these two bird species diverged from each other about 25 million years ago therefore means that the hepadnavirus is at least 25 million years old.
168	STEM: Which one of the following, if true, most strengthens the argument?
169	(A) [27% | Weak / No Impact] Viruses can affect the evolution of an organism and can thereby influence the likelihood of their diverging into two species.
170	(B) [15% | No Impact] The chromosomes of the zebra finch and the dark-eyed junco contain fragments of no virus other than the hepadnavirus.
171	(C) [47% | Correct] When a virus inserts itself into an animal’s chromosome, the insertion occurs at a random spot.
172	(D) [2% | Weak Impact] Many bird species other than the zebra finch and the dark-eyed junco contain fragments of the hepadnavirus.
173	(E) [9% | Irrelevant: species' survival] The presence of a hepadnavirus in an animal species does not affect the likelihood of that species’ survival.
174	--- BREAKDOWN
175	## Conclusion
176	The hepadnavirus is at least 25 million years old.
177	## Evidence
178	Once a hep virus inserts itself into a chromosome, fragments of the virus get passed to all its descendants. A hep virus fragment is found in precisely the same location in a certain chromosome of the junco and the zebra finch, and these two species diverged about 25 million years ago.
179	## Evaluate
180	This argument is hard to understand, let alone critique. The author is assuming that the junco and the finch had a common ancestor (more than 25 million years ago, before the two species diverged), that a hep virus inserted itself into a certain spot of a certain chromosome in this common ancestor, and that this is reason why we now see the junco and the finch having a hep virus fragment in the same spot of the same chromosome. We could use a Curious Fact / Explanation mindset to get through this problem.
181	  Curious Fact: Why does the junco and finch both have a hep virus fragment in the exact same spot of the same chromosome?
182	  Author's Explanation: The junco and the finch had a common ancestor; the hep virus inserted itself into the common ancestor and since the hep virus gets passed down to all of an animal's descendants, now the junco and finch both have it.
183	  We affect these arguments by either
184	  - discussing alternative explanations for the curious fact
185	  - affecting the plausibility of the author's explanation
186	  What could be a different explanation for why the junco and the finch have a hep virus fragment in the same spot? Couldn't it be that it didn't come from some common ancestor, but rather it came from two different ancestors, each of which had the hep virus insert itself into the same spot of the same chromosome?
187	## Goal
188	This answer could come in 3 different flavors:
189	  - rule out an alternate explanation for how the junco / finch have hep virus in the same spot
190	  - increase the plausibility that they have it in the same spot because of a common ancestor
191	  or
192	  - make it more likely that the hep virus is at least 25 million years old (maybe we find it in the fossils of animals that went extinct 30 million years ago)
193	--- CHOICE NOTES
194	(A) The strength of wording like "some / sometimes / can / not all" means that one example is enough to make it true. This sort of weak wording is almost always wrong on Strengthen, Weaken, and Paradox.
195	This answer seems to be trying to strengthen a connection between getting the hep virus and diverging into two species (so if there were a common ancestor to the junco/finch that got the hep virus, the hep virus may have influenced that species diverging 25 million years ago).
196	But the weak strength of the answer makes it unappealing. We have no idea if the hep virus is one of the viruses that can influence the likelihood, and even if it were one of them, it's not a great answer. We don't need to be convinced that the junco/finch's common ancestor diverged into two species. We already know that that happened 25 million years ago.
197	The fact that they diverged certainly doesn't prove that the species from which they came had a virus.
198	(B) Whether the chromosomes do or don't have other virus fragments doesn't change this conversation at all. We know they both have the hep virus fragment; we know that the hep virus fragment gets sent down the lineage to all of an animal's descendants; and the author can still make the same argument whether or not either animal has other fragments lying around.
199	(C) This is a brutal correct answer, but it's helping to rule out the alternative explanation that the junco and the finch each got their hep virus from a different ancestor.
200	If the hep virus inserts itself randomly, then it would be a 1 in a million chance that two organisms would magically get the hep virus inserted into precisely the same location of the same chromosome.
201	Since the junco/finch have the fragment at the same exact spot, it would be almost unthinkable that they got it separately. It makes it much more likely that they got it from a common ancestor who passes it along to all its descendants in that same exact spot.
202	Consider how the flipside of this answer would weaken — if we heard that "when a virus inserts into a chromosome, it always inserts into the gap between chromosome 19 and 20", then that would make it very possible that the junco and the finch both got their virus fragment from different sources.
203	(D) This might seem to somewhat strengthen, if we think that "if all these birds are getting the hep virus, then maybe they all got it from some common bird ancestor more than 25 million years ago".
204	But the author's case is that the junco and finch must have had a common ancestor, because they have the fragment at precisely the same location of the same chromosome, not because they're both birds.
205	This answer choice doesn't do anything for us unless we know that these other bird species also have the fragment in the same spot, as well as whether these other bird species also diverged from the junco/finch at least 25 million years ago.
206	(E) Nothing in this argument would be changed by whether the hep virus helps a species, hurts it, or is neutral to its survival likelihood.
207

209	=================== PT139-S1-Q13 | diff 2 | key B | score 22.8 | features: Alternatives, Argue the Anti-Conclusion, Defender
210	STIMULUS: A small collection of copper-alloy kitchen implements was found in an abandoned Roman-era well. Beneath them was a cache of coins, some of which dated to 375 A.D. The implements, therefore, were dropped into the well no earlier than 375 A.D.
211	STEM: Which one of the following, if true, most strengthens the argument?
212	(A) [5% | Very Weak Impact] The coins used in the Roman Empire often remained in circulation for many decades.
213	(B) [81% | Correct] The coins were found in a dense cluster that could not have been formed by coins slipping through an accumulation of larger objects.
214	(C) [1% | No Impact: value] The coins had far more value than the kitchen implements did.
215	(D) [7% | No Impact] The items in the well were probably thrown there when people evacuated the area and would have been retrieved if the people had returned.
216	(E) [6% | Weakens] Items of jewelry found beneath the coins were probably made around 300 A.D.
217	--- BREAKDOWN
218	## Conclusion (therefore)
219	The copper-alloy implements were dropped into the well no earlier than 375 A.D.
220	## Evidence
221	They were found in a well, and beneath them was a cache of coins, some of which dated to 375 A.D.
222	## Evaluate
223	How does the author think that the date of these coins tells us anything about the date of the kitchen implements? She's thinking, "if you drop something in a well, it lands on top of whatever is already in there. So the stuff farther down is older; the stuff farther up is newer." The coins were from as recently as 375 A.D., so, she reasons, anything on higher up in the well than the coins would have been dropped in after the coins, and thus after 375 A.D. How can we fight this logic? How could we argue "even if I accept your premise, you conclusion could be wrong"? Given that ... the coins below the copper implements were deposited as recently as 375 A.D, How could we still argue that ... the copper implements themselves were deposited earlier than 375 A.D.? We could say that, "unlike bulkier objects, coins can drop through to the bottom. So maybe those copper implements were dropped in the well around 100 A.D., and then 275 years later (in the year 375 A.D.) someone comes along, makes a wish on one of these coins, and tosses it in the well. Couldn't that coin drop beneath the copper implements, even though it was dropped in later?"
224	## Goal
225	Let's look for something that rules out our objection of "couldn't the coins have been deposited later and just fallen down beneath the copper implements" or look for an answer that finds some other way to increase the plausibility that these implements were no older than 375 A.D.
226	--- CHOICE NOTES
227	(A) It's unclear what the argument means by "the coins were dated to 375 A.D."
228	If it means, like modern day coins, "the coins were marked with the date 375 A.D. to signify the year they were minted", then this answer would mean that a coin made in 375 A.D. would still probably have been around 50 years later, so maybe someone in 425 A.D. still had this coin.
229	If it means, we did radio-carbon dating to see how old this coin was and we could tell it was as old as 375 A.D., then that doesn't tell us exactly when it was minted, so this answer doesn't have any direct impact.
230	In either case, this answer suggests that a coin that originated in 375 A.D. might still have been in someone's pocket 30-50 years after that, when they decided to chuck it in the well. If they didn't throw the coin into the well until even later, like 400 A.D., then that kind of strengthens the conclusion.
231	It sounds like we could say, "no earlier than 375 A.D.? This coin probably wasn't thrown in until 400 A.D., so the kitchen implements are probably younger than 400 A.D.", which would agree with the conclusion.
232	But this answer is just a very weak, general statement and so it's hard to draw any meaningful inference about whether this tells us anything about the coins in the well. We already know that one of them is at least as recent as 375 A.D. We don't really get any new strengthening value by saying, "Actually one of them is at least as recent as 390 A.D."
233	(B) This rules out the one big objection we could make to the argument. The author is assuming, "the lower in the well, the longer ago you were thrown in there. The higher in the well, the sooner you were thrown in there."
234	She thinks that if the coins are below the kitchen implements then the coins were thrown in the well before the kitchen implements were, but as we were thinking, "could a coin be thrown in a well and slip beneath the things that are already in there?"
235	This answer rules out that line of objection. It's saying, "this pile of coins could not have resulted from people throwing in coins after the kitchen implements had already been tossed in the well; this is not a pile of coins that could have slipped through the cracks in the pile of kitchen implements (larger objects) above it.
236	When we see these Defender style correct answers in Strengthen (they are ruling-out an objection), it reminds us that it can be very valuable to try to argue with a Strengthen argument the way we do when we're going Flaw and Weaken (and sometimes Necessary Assumption).
237	Sometimes we need to understand how the case being made is vulnerable to criticism, in order for us to understand how to strengthen the case.
238	(C) It doesn't make any difference whether the coins or the kitchen implements were more valuable than the other. We're only trying to sort out the chronological order in which they were tossed in the well.
239	(D) This tells us a potential backstory for how a bunch of objects ended up in a well, but this doesn't help us sort out the chronological ordering of "which was tossed in first: the coins or the kitchen implements"?
240	(E) This totally upends the author's assumption that "lower = older / higher = more recent".
241	This is giving us an example of a lower object that is younger than the higher object. The jewelry (younger) is lower in the well than the coins (which are older).
242	If we applied that logic to the kitchen implements, we could say that "even though the implements are higher in the well than the coins, they still might be older than the coins."
243

245	=================== PT141-S2-Q22 | diff 5 | key A | score 22.7 | features: Explain Curious Fact, Alternatives, Worst of the Worst
246	STIMULUS: Because the native salmon in Lake Clearwater had nearly disappeared, sockeye salmon were introduced in 1940. After being introduced, this genetically uniform group of sockeyes split into two distinct populations that do not interbreed, one inhabiting deep areas of the lake and the other inhabiting shallow areas. Since the two populations now differ genetically, some researchers hypothesize that each has adapted genetically to its distinct habitat.
247	STEM: Which of the following, if true, most strongly supports the researchers’ hypothesis?
248	(A) [48% | Correct] Neither of the two populations of sockeyes has interbred with the native salmon.
249	(B) [30% | No Impact] When the native salmon in Lake Clearwater were numerous, they comprised two distinct populations that did not interbreed.
250	(C) [8% | No Impact] Most types of salmon that inhabit lakes spend part of the time in shallow water and part in deeper water.
251	(D) [11% | Opposite (if anything)] One of the populations of sockeyes is virtually identical genetically to the sockeyes originally introduced in 1940.
252	(E) [2% | No Impact] The total number of sockeye salmon in the lake is not as large as the number of native salmon had been many years ago.
253	--- BREAKDOWN
254	## Hypothesis
255	The two populations of sockeye salmon differ genetically because they have each adapted genetically to their distinct habitat.
256	## Evidence
257	In 1940 a genetically uniform group of sockeyes was introduced into the lake. Now there's a group in the deep part of the lake and a group in shallow part, who don't interbreed and now differ genetically.
258	## Evaluate
259	As soon as we see the word *hypothesis* (a causal explanation for a phenomenon) in the question stem, we know we can use a Curious Fact / Explanation template.
260	*Curious Fact:* Why are the deep water sockeye now genetically different from the shallow sockeye?
261	*Author's Story:* They have each adapted genetically to their distinct habitat
262	We then evaluate this via two lenses:
263	1. is there some OTHER WAY to explain the curious fact?
264	2. how plausible is the AUTHOR'S WAY of explaining it?
265	Usually Strengthen questions deal with plausibility of the author's way. Is it plausible that each sockeye population has adapted genetically to its distinct habitat?
266	Can evolution actually work that quickly? Normally the genetic drift of a species takes thousands or millions of years, not the 80 years since 1940. The only time that evolution works super quickly is with a very isolated population and a very distinct selection pressure (like, all of a sudden, there's sulfur in the water and only the fish that mutate a gene that tolerates sulfur will survive and pass on their genes, so within a few generations, the sulfur-tolerant fish dominate and effectively the "genes" of that species have been changed).
267	To strengthens the AUTHOR'S STORY, we would want to know that the deep lake and the shallow lake have very different and very important selection pressures, in order for us to think that over the course of 80 years we would have had a mutation so strongly selected for that the populations of sockeye are now genetically different.
268	Is there some OTHER WAY to explain how these once genetically identical populations of sockeye are now different? Hmmm, how can you change genetic makeup, other than by mutation / natural selection? The only other way that genes get affected is through mating. When two animals have sex, their offspring is a genetic blend of each of the component animals. So maybe the deep lake sockeye and shallow lake sockeye now differ from each other because at least one of them is mating with some other animals, thereby changing the gene pool.
269	## Goal
270	We either need something that strengthens the idea that the two different parts of the lake have such different survival needs that it's plausible that evolutionary selection pressure could have changed the genetic makeup of the two populations over just 80 years,
271	or we need something that rules out an alternate explanation for why the two populations now have different genetic makeup.
272	--- CHOICE NOTES
273	(A) This rules out some OTHER WAY that their genetics would have changed. If the deep lake sockeye mated with the native salmon, then the result would be fish that have 1/2 sockeye genes, 1/2 native genes.
274	
275	If this were happening in the deep lake, but not the shallow lake, then 80 years later we'd see the deep lake sockeye have a different genetic makeup, since there would now be a lot of native salmon genes mixed into their [ahem] gene *pool.*
276	
277	This answer doesn't totally rule out interbreeding as the explanation for the genetic change (maybe some of the sockeye interbred with an animal other than the native salmon), but it's incredibly unlikely if not impossible that it happened with a different animal. Usually two species are defined as distinct *because* they can't interbreed. So salmon should only be able to interbreed with other salmon, one would think.
278	
279	If interbreeding didn't change the genetic makeup, then we're more likely to accept the author's explanation that adaptation changed the genetic makeup.
280	(B) Citing a parallel between sockeye and native in terms of the fact that the deep lake crew doesn't associate with the shallow lake crew isn't doing anything for us in terms of ruling out an Alternate Explanation or boosting the plausibility of the Author's Explanation.
281	(C) We don't really care what most types of salmon do. The fact that most salmon would hang out in both parts of the lake, whereas sockeye are segregating themselves into two camps, doesn't rule out an Alternate Explanation for the genetic change nor does it help the Author's Explanation.
282	(D) This answer potentially weakens, since the author's conclusion is that "each population has adapted genetically to its distinct habitat". This answer makes it seem like one of the two populations has not adapted genetically to its habitat. It sounds like their DNA is basically the same as when they were dumped there in 1940.
283	(E) The total number of fish in the lake doesn't help us assess the causal mystery behind why the two populations of sockeye now have different genetic makeups.
284

286	=================== PT142-S1-Q2 | diff 2 | key C | score 22.0 | features: Explain Curious Fact, Plausibility
287	STIMULUS: Archaeologist: How did the Parthenon’s stonemasons
288	manage to carve columns that all bulged outward
289	in the center in precisely the same way? One
290	hypothesis is suggested by the discovery of a
291	scale drawing of a column etched into the stone
292	of a Greek temple at Didyma. The drawing is
293	a profile view of a column surrounded by a
294	grid, which makes it possible to determine the
295	correct width at every height of the column.
296	The stonemasons who carved the Parthenon’s
297	columns may have relied on a drawing like the
298	one at Didyma.
299	STEM: Which one of the following, if true, adds the most
300	support for the archaeologist’s hypothesis?
301	(A) [1% | No Impact: modern attempts] Modern attempts to recreate columns like those at the Parthenon have only been partially successful.
302	(B) [4% | Weakens Very Slightly] The construction of the temple at Didyma was begun over a century after the Parthenon was constructed.
303	(C) [87% | Correct] Scale drawings were commonly used in many types of construction in ancient Greece.
304	(D) [3% | Unclear Impact] The surviving columns at Didyma are almost twice as tall as the columns at the Parthenon.
305	(E) [5% | Weakens, if anything] The Parthenon’s stonemasons had considerable experience carving columns before they started work on the Parthenon.
306	--- BREAKDOWN
307	## Curious Fact
308	How did the Parthenon's builders manage to carve columns that all bulged outward in the center in the exactly the same way?
309	## Hypothesis
310	They relied on a scale drawing like the one at Didyma (it's a profile view of a column surrounded by a grid, which makes it possible to determine the correct width at every height of the column).
311	## Evaluate
312	Whenever the question stem uses the noun hypothesis (rather than argument / reasoning), we know going into the paragraph that the author will be trying to Explain a Curious Fact (i.e. trying to solve a causal mystery).
313	We find the curious fact and ask it as a question, although in this case it actually was presented as a question!
314	We find the author's explanation / hypothesis and make sure we understand the causal story they're telling.
315	Then we think along two different dimensions:
316	1. Could there be some Alternate Explanation for the Curious Fact?
317	2. How plausible is the Author's Explanation?
318	How else could they have carved columns that bulged outward in precisely the same way?
319	Hmm. Maybe someone stood back 100 yds to get a good view of all the columns and yelled through a bullhorn, "Take a little more off column 3!" Maybe they created a giant mold for the columns, so every column came out the same because it was mixed and hardened in the same mold.
320	Honestly, for a question as technical as "how did they get columns to bulge identically in the center", LSAT doesn't expect us to have any common sense imagination for Alternate Explanations. If they haven't given us any crumbs of a hint or if it's not the sort of situation that has a common sense hypothesis attached to it, then they probably aren't testing Alternate Explanations.
321	How plausible is the notion that they used a scale drawing like the one at Didyma?
322	Are the two construction projects similar enough that the same tactic could have worked for both, or are there important differences where you could use a scale drawing for Didyma but not for the Parthenon?
323	Did the stonemasons who worked on the Parthenon have the same knowledge and training that those who worked on Didyma did? Were they familiar with this scale drawing technique?
324	## Goal
325	Like most Explain the Curious Fact arguments, we don't have a specific prediction.
326	Since this is Strengthen, we will justify our answer by saying it either
327	1. rules out an Alternate Explanation (for how they got the columns to bulge similarly)
328	or
329	2. adds plausibility to the Author's Explanation (that they used a scale drawing like the one at Didyma)
330	Strengthen questions are more likely to add plausibility than to rule out an alternate explanation, so on a first pass, we primarily look for answers that seem to do #2.
331	--- CHOICE NOTES
332	(A) It really doesn't make any difference how successful *modern* attempts have / haven't been.
333	
334	If we said that a modern team *tried to use a scale drawing* like the one at Didyma and couldn't seem to get the columns to bulge identically, that would weaken the plausibility that a scale drawing was the correct explanation of how the Parthenon was built.
335	(B) This somewhat detracts from the plausibility of the author's explanation. If Didyma happened over a century after the Parthenon, it opens up the possibility that at the time of the Parthenon's construction, these scale drawings didn't even exist yet.
336	(C) This adds to the plausibility of the Author's Explanation. He thinks that they used a scale drawing like the one at Didyma to get those matching column bulges.
337	
338	This answer says that scale drawings were commonly used in many types of construction. Okay, so it sounds like that could be a correct guess. The fact that Didyma was a temple (whereas the Parthenon is a government building, I believe) might have been a concern. Are these buildings too different to have employed the same building method? But learning that scale drawings were common in many types of construction increases the odds that the people building the Parthenon were familiar with scale drawings, had access to them, had seen them before, etc.
339	
340	On a first pass, I was not attracted to this, because it's very unspecific to the Parthenon. But it does more than nothing, and that's all it takes to win the contest of "most strengthens" if every other answer does nothing or goes the wrong way.
341	
342	If we're trying to figure out how Jessica killed Billy and our hypothesis is "she poisoned him with arsenic", it somewhat adds plausibility to that hypothesis to be told that "arsenic was commonly found in the pantry of many types of homes in the city where Jessica and Billy lived".
343	(D) Some people might argue this weakens, because it makes the construction project at Didyma seem significantly different than the one at the Parthenon.
344	
345	The sort of techniques you use to build a skyscraper might be different from the ones you use to build a building half its size.
346	
347	Other people might think this strengthens, in the sense of "If scale drawings could handle the challenge of super tall Didyma, then they must have been capable of handling the more modest challenge of the Parthenon".
348	
349	The fact that we could argue it weakens or strengthens illustrates that it has no clear impact.
350	(E) This doesn't suggest anything about scale drawings, so it's not adding plausibility to the author's explanation.
351	
352	It almost seems to be suggesting an Alternate Explanation for how the masons got the matching column bulges -- they were just wicked good masons with lots of column-carving experience.

355	=================== PT148-S3-Q23 | diff 5 | key A | score 22.0 | features: Explain Curious Fact, Science
356	STIMULUS: Scientist: Some colonies of bacteria produce antibiotic molecules called phenazines, which they use to fend off other bacteria. We hypothesize that phenazines also serve as molecular pipelines that give interior bacteria access to essential nutrients in the environment surrounding the colony.
357	STEM: Which one of the following, if true, provides the most support for the scientist’s hypothesis?
358	(A) [48% | Correct] Bacteria colonies that do not produce phenazines form wrinkled surfaces, thus increasing the number of bacteria that are in direct contact with the surrounding environment.
359	(B) [10% | No Impact] The rate at which a bacteria colony produces phenazines is determined by the number of foreign bacteria in the environment immediately surrounding the colony.
360	(C) [10% | Weakens, if anything] When bacteria colonies that do not produce phenazines are buried in nutrient-rich soil, they grow as quickly as colonies that do produce phenazines.
361	(D) [24% | No Impact] Bacteria colonies that produce phenazines are better able to fend off other bacteria than are bacteria colonies that do not produce phenazines.
362	(E) [8% | Unclear Impact] Within bacteria colonies that produce phenazines, interior bacteria are more likely to die than are bacteria along the edges.
363	--- BREAKDOWN
364	## Conclusion / Hypothesis
365	Phenazines serve as molecular pipelines that give interior bacteria access to essential nutrients in the environment surrounding the colony.
366	## Evaluate
367	There isn't any support provided for this hypothesis, so we're not evaluating an argument. Normally when we see the word 'hypothesis' we use our Curious Fact / Explanation mindset.
368	
369	Here, the Curious Fact would just be, "What the heck are these phenazine things all about?"
370	
371	However, we already have one valid answer -- they're antibiotic molecules that fend off other bacteria. The scientist's hypothesis is speculating an *additional* possible explanation for phenazines.
372	
373	Usually with Curious Fact / Explanation problems, we're thinking about affecting the argument in two different ways:
374	- considering Alternate Explanations for the curious fact
375	- considering the plausibility of the Author's Explanation
376	
377	But since we already have an explanation for phenazines, we wouldn't waste any time worrying, "Could there be some *other* reason for phenazines?" We already know that fending off bacteria is one reason.
378	
379	So all we're concerned with is the plausibility of the author's additional explanation -- that phenazines act like pipelines that give interior bacteria access to essential nutrients in the environment surrounding the colony?
380	
381	Picture a bacterial growth on a piece of moldy bread. It ends up looking like a big oval type shape. That whole shape is full of molecules of bacterium. The colony grows outward as it consumes more nutrients that facilitate its growth. But how do the molecules of bacterium on the inside of the colony get food? They're just surrounded by more bacteria, not by more bread to consume.
382	
383	According to this scientist, phenazines are taking the nutrients from the not-yet-moldy bread on the exterior of the colony and shuttling it into the interior of the moldy spot in order to feed the bacteria living at "city center" (it's almost like when metropolitan cities are surrounded by a Beltway, and there are some roads that lead from the Beltway to the city center).
384	## Goal
385	We just need an answer that makes it seem somewhat more plausible that phenazines act like a pipeline that shuttles nutrients from outside the moldy circle to the inside of the circle.
386	
387	The most common way that LSAT strengthens the argument is "No Cause, No Effect", so that would mean something like, "When there aren't phenazines, there *isn't* the piping of nutrients from the outside to the inside."
388	--- CHOICE NOTES
389	(A) This answer is using the classic "No Cause, No Effect" type structure, but it's pretty confusing to process.
390	
391	The "problem" that phenazines are supposedly solving is, "How do we get nutrients from the outside of the colony to the inside? The bacteria in the inside of the colony are trapped. They're surrounded by other bacteria in the colony. They can't eat their own colony. So we have to pass them food from the exterior".
392	
393	If the bacteria has a wrinkled surface, then more of the colony is in direct contact with the surrounding environment. We don't have bacteria that are trapped. Most / all of them have some direct contact with the surrounding environment, so they can get their own food.
394	
395	Thus, since they don't have the problem of "how do we get food to the bacteria that has no direct contact with the environment", they don't have the evolutionary "solution" of phenazine pipelines.
396	(B) This doesn't give us any reason to think these things work as pipelines to get food from the exterior of the colony to the trapped bacteria on the inside.
397	
398	Instead, this answer reinforces the other function of phenazines, defending against foreign bacteria. This answer is showing how phenazine production is proportional to "need to defend against foreign bacteria".
399	
400	Since the growth of phenazine is directly tied to this other function, if anything this answer would weaken the notion that phenazine is also integral as a food pipeline (this answer suggests that if there were no foreign bacteria in the surrounding environment, there might not even be phenazines).
401	(C) If phenazines were nutrient-pipelines that help feed the starving centers of bacteria colonies, then we would expect colonies with phenazines to be more successful than colonies without.
402	
403	But according to this answer, if you throw both types of bacteria into nutrient-filled soil, they do about equally well.
404	(D) This is similar to (B). It's related to the known function of phenazines — fending off bacteria. This answer provides nothing that helps support the idea that phenazines also perform the function of being a nutrient pipeline to the city-center of the bacteria colony.
405	(E) On one hand, maybe we could say, "See? The hypothesis is right. These bacteria that have phenazines have a problem with interior bacteria starving because they're trapped from nutrients!"
406	
407	But on the other hand, we could say, "Hmmm, looks like the hypothesis is wrong. Supposedly the phenazine should be piping nutrients to these interior bacteria, but it looks like they're not, since those interior bacteria are dying."
408	
409	I think LSAC intended this more as a Weaken idea.

412	=================== PT112-S3-Q26 | diff 4 | key B | score 21.8 | features: Explain Curious Fact, Defender, Plausibility, Science
413	STIMULUS: Before 1986 physicists believed they could describe the universe in terms of four universal forces. Experiments then suggested, however, a fifth universal force of mutual repulsion between particles of matter. This fifth force would explain the occurrence in the experiments of a smaller measurement of the gravitational attraction between bodies than the established theory predicted.
414	STEM: Which one of the following, if true, most strengthens the argument that there is a fifth universal force?
415	(A) [14% | Almost No Impact] The extremely sophisticated equipment used for the experiments was not available to physicists before the 1970s.
416	(B) [71% | Correct] No previously established scientific results are incompatible with the notion of a fifth universal force.
417	(C) [5% | Weakens, if anything] Some scientists have suggested that the alleged fifth universal force is an aspect of gravity rather than being fundamental in itself.
418	(D) [8% | Weakens] The experiments were conducted by physicists in remote geological settings in which factors affecting the force of gravity could not be measured with any degree of precision.
419	(E) [3% | No Impact] The fifth universal force was postulated at a time in which many other exciting and productive ideas in theoretical physics were developed.
420	--- BREAKDOWN
421	## Curious Fact
422	Why is there less gravitational attraction between bodies than our theories predict?
423	## Explanation
424	Maybe there's a 5th universal force, which involves mutual repulsion between particles of matter.
425	## Evaluate
426	Whenever we're trying to Solve a Causal Mystery and we have a potential hypothesis to do so, we're supposed to consider two different questions:
427	1. is there some **Alternate Way** we could explain / interpret the curious fact? 2. how **plausible** is the Author's Way of explaining it?
428	How else could we explain why the gravitational attraction between bodies is measured smaller than the predicted amount?
429	- maybe gravity isn't as strong as we think it is? - maybe the measuring devices were off? - maybe there's some interaction with the other 3 known forces that would explain it?
430	How plausible is the idea that there is a 5th force that deals with mutual repulsion between particles of matter?
431	- do any other experiments suggest such a force? - would such a force resolve any other physical mysteries we have? - is there any mathematical modeling of such a force?
432	## Goal
433	Since this is Strengthen, we want to either **rule out** an alternate way to explain the curious fact (e.g. "the measurements **aren't** smaller than predicted because gravity is just weaker than we thought / because our measurements were off") or **increase** the plausibility of the presented explanation, that there is fifth force that involves mutual repulsion between particles of matter.
434	--- CHOICE NOTES
435	(A) Maybe this answer makes us feel slightly more trust in the accuracy of the measurements? It would be harder to say, "the real reason that gravitational attraction was measured as less than predicted is because the equipment we used to measure it was busted", if we know that the scientists are using extremely sophisticated equipment that isn't even 20 years old.
436	
437	But saying, "this equipment wasn't available before the 1970s" doesn't really tell us anything. Who cares if it was available? It doesn't really matter; we just want to know if we can trust the measurements, and this answer doesn't address the accuracy of the measurements.
438	(B) This weakly strengthens, but it ends up being the strongest answer, since everything else does nothing or goes the wrong way.
439	
440	If we are considering positing a brand new force to explain these mysterious measurements, we would hope that it wouldn't be contradicted by any of our established existing science.
441	
442	This is really just a Necessary Assumption. Whenever the correct answer on Strengthen is a Necessary Assumption, it feels pretty weak (because it is). But you can feel how bad it would be if you negated the answer.
443	
444	If some previously **established** results would be incompatible ( **contradictory** ) with the notion of this 5th force, that would greatly, greatly diminish the plausibility of this 5th force theory.
445	
446	Overall, saying that this new hypothesis doesn't contradict anything that goes before it is not really positive support for it; it's just saying there's no big negative evidence against it.
447	
448	That probably seems like a very weak plausibility strengthener, and it is, but because we're talking about fundamental forces of the universe, it's slightly more impressive than normal.

522	Poor nutrition is at the root of the violent behavior of many young offenders.
523	## Intermediate Conclusion
524	The results from this prison study confirm a link between poor nutrition and violent behavior.
525	## Evidence

527	In another study, some violent inmates were switched onto a diet high in nutrients, and there was a steady improvement in their behavior over the four months of the experiment.
528	## Evaluate
529	Since the argument is interpreting the causal meaning behind the results of a study, we should use our Explain Curious Fact framework.
530	Curious Fact ... Why is it that there was a steady improvement in the behavior of violent inmates during the four months after they were placed on a diet high in nutrients?
531	Author's Explanation ... Poor nutrition causes violent behavior, so once we improved their nutrition, the violent behavior started to go away.
532	We always evaluate these via two different lines of inquiry: 1. Could there be an Alternate Explanation for the curious fact? 2. How **plausible** is the Author's Interpretation?
533	Can we think of other reasons why the behavior would have improved among these violent inmates who were placed on the high nutrition diet?
534	Maybe it's a reverse causality sort of thing --- the people who are willing to be rehabilitated in prison are more likely to agree to be placed on a high nutrient diet in an experiment. Being placed on the experimental diet didn't cause their behavior to improve. Their willingness to improve is what led them to participate in the experiment.
535	Maybe it's just a case that four months in prison mellows you out. You came into prison a violent offender, but once you see how badly you get disciplined if you act out in prison, you start to fall in line (that would be classified as an "improvement in their behavior").
536	How plausible is the idea that poor nutrition leads to violent behavior? Is there any physiological explanation that could help us understand that causal mechanism (i.e. do nutrients give us the will power to resist doing bad things?) Are there any more data points that show good behavior correlated with good nutrition or bad behavior correlated with bad nutrition?
537	## Goal
538	Since this is Strengthen, we're more likely to see an answer that boosts the plausibility of the Author's Explanation. The most common form of correct answer on Strengthen (for Explain Curious Fact arguments) is a No Cause, No Effect answer, like "When the inmates were taken **off** the high nutrient diet, their bad behavior came back".
539	But a correct answer could also rule out an alternate explanation for why their behavior improved, like "Being in an experiment does not itself give someone a sense of self-worth that leads to better behavior".

If we found out that the adopted children of parents who had perfect pitch did not have perfect pitch, that would be strong evidence that perfect pitch is genetic. Perhaps they're on a topic not previously addressed by Epictetus. Thus, there would be no basis for people noticing a mismatch between what they knew to be Epictetus's view on a matter and what view this book was attributing to Epictetus. - Maybe Arrian's views actually aligned so much with Epictetus' that no one reading the book who already knew Epictetus's views would find anything that seemed noticeably different.

654	## Conclusion (we can conclude)

660	This argument has a couple layers, so it's hard to know what to focus on. It has some Explain Curious Fact, and it has some Argument by Analogy.

664	  The other layer here is the way the author seems to connect an ability to recognize dominance in chimps to the ability to recognize extroversion in humans. She's trying to make the case that since we have the ability to recognize X in a human face and Y in a chimp's face, then our ability to recognize X must be a primate thing.
665	  The reason she's saying "it's a primate thing", rather than just "a human thing", is because she thinks that recognizing X and recognizing Y are somehow part of the same story.

1046	
1047	the higher the volume setting, the louder the music
1048

1050	
1051	the lower the volume setting, the softer the music

This one is the latter. We can use the hypothesis to figure out what would be the Curious Question that this hypothesis would be answering. Curious Question: How is it that seals distinguish between the harmful killer-whales and the harmless killer-whales? Hypothesis: They start off afraid of all killer whales but then learn to ignore the harmless ones. 1233	In a study of stroke patients, there was a correlation between those with long-term brain damage and those with highest levels of glutamate in their blood.
1234	## Evaluate
1235	Since the evidence involves a study with a correlation (people who were X had the highest levels of Y) and the conclusion involves causality (Y is a cause of X), we can use our Curious Fact / Explanation template.
1236	*Curious Fact*
1237	Why did the stroke patients with the long term damage to their brain's nerve cells have the highest levels of glutamate in their blood?
1238	*Author's Explanation*
1239	Glutamate leaked from damaged or oxygen-starved nerve cells and killed surrounding nerve cells.
1240	We now start asking questions like
1241	- can we think of some Alternate Explanation for why the patients with brain damage had high levels of glutamate in their blood?
1242	- how plausible is the Author's Explanation?
1243	With correlations, we usually consider Alternate Explanations like reverse causality or third factor.
1244	Is it possible the brain damage came first, and the high glutamate level came second? Seems hard to argue in that fashion, but maybe something else caused the deteriorating nerve cells, and once the damage had been done, the glutamate leaked out into the brain.
1245	Could there be some third factor that is separately causing both? Sure, maybe, there's some underlying genetic trait that makes these patients more likely to suffer from brain damage and high glutamate levels.
1246	In terms of the plausibility of the author's explanation, it would help the author's story if we established that these patients with brain damage could have had damaged or oxygen-starved nerve cells. It would also help to know that the high glutamate level came before the brain damage.
1247	The most common way to strengthen causality is No Cause, No Effect, so we might see an answer that says "Stroke patients with the least amount of glutamate did not have continuing deterioration."
1248	## Goal
1249	Find an answer that rules out a different explanation for the correlation between "continuing deterioration and high glutamate" or that adds some plausibility to the author's explanation.
1250	--- CHOICE NOTES
1251	(A) We didn't need to be convinced that glutamate will damage surrounding nerve cells (we had already been told that), so this is really only opening up the possibility of **alternate causes**, if there are other neurotransmitters that also could be the culprit in causing this brain damage.
1252	(B) A "wide variety of abnormal chemicals in their blood" is too unspecific to do anything. At worst, this is actually nudging in a weaken direction by suggesting that there might be other possible chemical levels that are off, and one of those might be causing the brain damage.
1253	(C) This is better than nothing, kind of, although we really don't care if other neurotransmitters leak. We'd only care if the other neurotransmitters were known to kill nerve cells (then they would be a potential alternate cause).
1254	(D) "the only" is a sufficient condition indicator (unlike "only / only if" which are both necessary indicators).
1255	
1256	```
1257	Glutamate           it leaked from damaged or
1258	in the blood   →    oxygen-starved nerve cells
1259	```
1260	
1261	We now know that the stroke patients in this study, the ones that had the highest levels of glutamate, definitely got that because glutamate leaked from damaged or oxygen-starved nerve cells.
1262	
1263	Wait, didn't we already know that? [Check the text]
1264	
1265	No, sneaky-sneaky. It was phrased as a conditional, with the if trigger hidden by putting it later in the sentence.
1266	
1267	If glutamate leaks, then the glutamate can kill nearby nerve cells.
1268	
1269	But in these stroke patients, did the glutamate leak? We were never told it did. Obviously, in order for the author's brain damage story to be correct, he's assuming that some glutamate leaked out of struggling nerve cells.
1270	
1271	This answer strengthens by establishing that assumption, as well as simultaneously ruling out all other possible explanations for having glutamate in the blood.
1272	(E) Stuff as weakly worded as "some, can, may, sometimes" is almost always wrong on Strengthen, Weaken, Paradox.
1273	
1274	It's irrelevant to this argument whether the leaky cells are also killed. The author is only counting on glutamate killing surrounding cells.

1277	=================== PT148-S1-Q11 | diff 1 | key D | score 20.0 | features: Explain Curious Fact, Medical, Plans / Recommendations, Plausibility
1278	STIMULUS: A year ago several regional hospitals attempted to reduce the number of patient injuries resulting from staff errors by implementing a plan to systematically record all such errors. The incidence of these injuries has substantially decreased at these hospitals since then. Clearly, the knowledge that their errors were being carefully monitored made the hospitals’ staffs much more meticulous in carrying out their patient-care duties.
1279	STEM: Which one of the following, if true, most strengthens the argument?
1280	(A) [1% | Weakens, if anything] Before the plan was implemented the hospitals already had a policy of thoroughly investigating any staff error that causes life-threatening injury to a patient.
1281	(B) [0% | Weakens] The incidence of patient injuries at a regional hospital that did not participate in the plan also decreased over the year in question.
1282	(C) [1% | Weakens, if anything] The plan did not call for the recording of staff errors that could have caused patient injuries but did not.
1283	(D) [97% | Correct] The decrease in the incidence of the injuries did not begin at any hospital until the staff there became aware that the records were being closely analyzed.
1284	(E) [1% | Weakens, if anything] Under the plan, the hospitals’ staff members who were found to have made errors that caused injuries to patients received only reprimands for their first errors.
1285	--- BREAKDOWN
1286	## Conclusion (clearly)
1287	The knowledge that their errors were being monitored made the hospital staff more meticulous in carrying out their patient-care duties.
1288	## Evidence
1289	Ever since the hospital instituted a plan to record all staff errors, the rate of patient injuries resulting from such errors has gone down a bunch.
1290	## Evaluate
1291	There are a lot of copies of this same argument throughout the LSAT canon:
1292	  Premise 1: A policy change was made
1293	  Premise 2: A change has been observed.
1294	  Conclusion: The policy change was the reason
1295	  It might be, "We lowered the speed limit. Since then, highway fatalities are lower. Thus, the speed limit has made people drive more safely"
1296	  or it might be, "I started meditating last Friday. Since then, my Games sections have been going better. Thus, the meditation is helping!"
1297	  But whenever an LSAT author presents a Curious Fact and then concludes something that reveals a Causal Explanation being assumed, then we are supposed to worry about
1298	  1. Alternate Explanations for the Curious Fact
1299	  2. the plausibility of the Author's Explanation
1300	  Here, our curious fact is, "why have injuries due to staff errors gone down" and the causal explanation being assumed is that "we instituted this plan to systematically record such errors. The staff was aware of it and did not want their errors recorded and so has become much more meticulous in carrying out their patient-care duties, which has led to fewer injuries."
1301	## Goal
1302	Since this is Strengthen, we want to either
1303	  rule out an alternate explanation for the curious fact (e.g. "the incidence of injuries hasn't gone down because we fired Eddie, who was responsible for most of them, or because we just see fewer patients than before)
1304	  or
1305	  increase the plausibility of the author's explanation (e.g. "in the parts of the hospital where they don't record such errors, we haven't seen a reduction in patient injuries")
1306	--- CHOICE NOTES
1307	(A) This actually hurts the plausibility that this new plan to record errors is the Causal Difference-Maker that led to the reduction in injuries.
1308	
1309	After all, if there were already a lot of accountability for errors, then there was already a strong disincentive to messing up.
1310	(B) This decreases the plausibility that the Plan was the causal difference-maker and suggests that something else is happening in the region that is causing the injuries to go down.
1311	
1312	In the LSAT biz, we call the form of this plausibility-weakener
1313	No Cause, Effect
1314	of "Effect w/o Cause"
1315	
1316	The author is thinking
1317	
1318	```
1319	Cause                  Effect
1320	Plan to record errors  Fewer patient injuries
1321	```
1322	
1323	So we could strengthen by showing that where there was no plan (no cause), there was no improvement in patient injuries (no effect).
1324	
1325	Instead, this answer is giving us the weaken version of that, showing that at hospitals where there was no plan (No Cause) there was still a reduction in injuries (Effect).
1326	(C) This pretty much has no impact, because it's not a very impactful idea, but saying that staff members weren't recorded for close calls that could have resulted in injuries but luckily didn't means that the oversight wasn't as harsh as it could have been.
1327	
1328	The harsher the oversight, the more we're strengthening the author's story, that staff at the hospital were coerced into being more meticulous to prevent errors.
1329	
1330	Since this is making the oversight seem more lenient, it's going in a weakening direction.
1331	(D) If the injuries started decreasing before the staff even knew they were being surveilled, then that would kind of ruin the author's story. The author thinks that the staff's "knowledge that their errors were being carefully monitored" was the causal difference-maker, so he wouldn't be expecting there to be a reduction in injuries unless the staff was aware they were being monitored.
1332	
1333	This answer is pretty close to what we'll see on Necessary Assumption — it establishes a baseline of plausibility of the author's explanation.
1334	
1335	And it's similar to Necessary Assumption in the sense that the easiest way to like this answer is to think about how badly it would weaken the argument if this weren't the case.
1336	
1337	If we said that the injuries were decreasing before the staff even knew it was being monitored, that would be a compelling (No Cause, Effect) weakener.
1338	
1339	This answer is a weakly compelling (No Cause, No Effect) strengthener, because it's saying in the time period before the staff was aware they were being analyzed (No Cause) we hadn't yet seen a reduction in injuries (No Effect).
1340	
1341	When we're doing Strengthen on an Explain the Curious Fact type argument, the most common form the correct answer takes is some version of this No Cause / No Effect structure.
1342	
1343	In our write-up of the stimulus, we predicted a different version:
1344	in areas of the hospital where the plan was not used, there was no reduction in such injuries.
1345	(E) The harsher the oversight, the more we're strengthening the author's story, which is that staff at the hospital were coerced into being more meticulous because they knew their errors were being monitored and recorded.
1346	
1347	Since this is making the oversight seem more lenient (first offense was just a slap on the wrist), it's going in a weakening direction.
