1350	=================== PT148-S1-Q23 | diff 5 | key E | score 19.8 | features: Explain Curious Fact, Plans / Recommendations, Plausibility
1351	STIMULUS: Editorial: Teenagers tend to wake up around 8:00 A.M., the time when they stop releasing melatonin, and are sleepy if made to wake up earlier. Since sleepiness can impair driving ability, car accidents involving teenagers driving to school could be reduced if the school day began later than 8:00 A.M. Indeed, when the schedule for Granville’s high school was changed so that school began at 8:30 A.M. rather than earlier, the overall number of car accidents involving teenage drivers in Granville declined.
1352	STEM: Which one of the following, if true, provides the most support for the argument in the editorial?
1353	(A) [10% | No Impact] Teenagers start releasing melatonin later at night and stop releasing it later in the morning than do young children.
1354	(B) [18% | No Impact] Sleepy teenagers are tardy for school more frequently than teenagers who are well rested when the school day begins.
1355	(C) [3% | No Impact] Teenagers who work at jobs during the day spend more time driving than do teenagers who attend high school during the day.
1356	(D) [11% | Weakens, if anything] Many of the car accidents involving teenage drivers in Granville occurred in the evening rather than in the morning.
1357	(E) [59% | Correct] Car accidents involving teenage drivers rose in the region surrounding Granville during the time they declined in Granville.
1358	--- BREAKDOWN
1359	## Conclusion
1360	If the school day started later than 8am, car accidents involving teenagers driving to school could be reduced.
1361	## Evidence (since, indeed)
1362	  - Sleepiness can impair driving ability.
1363	  - Teens tend to wake up around 8am.
1364	  - If teens are made to wake up earlier, they are sleepy.
1365	  - When Granville's high school pushed back its starting time to 830am, the overall number of car accidents involving teen drivers declined in Granville.
1366	## Evaluate
1367	Well, this is a mouthful to take in. On one hand we have a Comparison situation because the author is proposing a certain Plan (later start time for school day) and predicting a certain Result/Goal (reduce accidents involving teens driving to school).
1368	  We have to consider differences between "Plan World" and "Actual World".
1369	  The author is highlighting one thing that will change, [teens are hopefully less sleepy while driving to school], but we need to consider what other things might change? Maybe the normal rush hour in Granville is from 8am - 9am, so if we are suddenly adding teen drivers to that mix, we're making that hour of driving more dangerous in a way that might actually add more accidents involving teen drivers. LSAT loves to test the idea of a Plan backfiring and making something worse in an unanticipated way. This Plan also assumes that it's feasible for teens to sleep in to 8am, regardless of school start time. Maybe their parents are waking them up by 7am
1370	  Remember, teens don't have to cause the accident. As long as their in an accident with some road-rage morning commuter, it still counts as "an accident involving teenagers".
1371	  There's also a conditional logic element to this answer. If teens are made to wake up earlier than 8, they are sleepy. Our author seems to be assuming a difference, that "if they are made to wake up after 8, they will be less/not sleepy."
1372	  Finally, there's the Interpreting a Curious Fact part of this argument. The author is assuming that the number of car accidents decreased when Granville's high school pushed its start time back is because there was less sleepy driving to school by teens. But maybe there's some alternate explanation for why number of car accidents went down (i.e. a pandemic where everyone has to stay home).
1373	## Goal
1374	Yikes, so how many pressure points does this argument have?
1375	  - we want to know that if schools pushed back their start times, it would be feasible for teens to wake up later, that they would do so, that they would be less sleepy as a result of doing so, and that changing their drive-to-school times later would not create any new issues that might facilitate more accidents involving teens.
1376	  - additionally, we're in the market for any answers ruling out alternate explanations for why teen accidents declined in Granville or making it more plausible that the change of start time for the high school was the reason.
1377	--- CHOICE NOTES
1378	(A) This argument is only about teens, their melatonin, their sleepiness, their accidents. Learning anything about young children's melatonin level is irrelevant to us.
1379	(B) If the conclusion were purely a recommendation that "we should push back start time", then this could pile on an additional reason why it might be worth doing (fewer tardy students). But the conclusion is only about whether accidents involving teens would rise or fall, and this answer has nothing to do with that.
1380	(C) This plan is changing the start time for high school, so it affects teens who attend high school during the day. Teens that don't go to high school during the day won't be directly affected by this plan, so making any sort of comparison to them is just as irrelevant as it was to bring up young children in (A).
1381	(D) Really, this answer is a non-issue. No one is assuming that all accidents involving teens occur in the morning, so learning that "many" occur in the evening really changes nothing that our common sense hadn't already assumed.
1382	
1383	But to the extent that this plan is meant to reduce accidents by focusing on morning driving, the more we think that teen accidents occur in the evening, the more we're weakening the potential of this Plan to impact the root of the problem.
1384	(E) It's important to remember how bad the other four answers are, because this doesn't have to do much to still be the answer that strengthens most. This increases the plausibility that the reduction of accidents in Granville was due to something local to Granville. If accidents are going up in surrounding regions, it implicitly rules out the possibility of broader alternative explanations like a pandemic / a weather catastrophe that led to less driving.
1385	
1386	If this answer is telling us that the thing that caused the decline in Granville was local to Granville, does that prove that it was the changed high school start time? Of course not, but it does add some plausibility to that while diminishing the plausibility of some more regional alternative explanations.

1454	=================== PT113-S3-Q5 | diff 3 | key E | score 19.5 | features: Predictions
1455	STIMULUS: Television executives recently announced that advertising time on television will cost 10 to 15 percent more next fall than it cost last fall. The executives argued that in spite of this increase, advertisers will continue to profit from television advertising, and so advertising time will be no harder to sell next fall than it was last fall.
1456	STEM: Which one of the following, if true, would most support the television executives' argument?
1457	(A) [9% | Weakens, if anything] Most costs of production and distribution of products typically advertised on television are expected to rise 3 to 7 percent in the next year.
1458	(B) [1% | Unclear Impact] The system for rating the size of the audience watching any given television advertisement will change next fall.
1459	(C) [6% | Weakens, if anything] Next fall advertising time on television will no longer be available in blocks smaller than 30 seconds.
1460	(D) [11% | Irrelevant Distinction] The amount of television advertising time purchased by providers of services is increasing, while the amount of such time purchased by providers of products is decreasing.
1461	(E) [73% | Correct] A recent survey has shown that the average number of hours people spend watching television is increasing at the rate of 2 percent every two months.
1462	--- BREAKDOWN
1463	## Conclusion (so)
1464	Advertising time will be no harder to sell next fall than it was last fall.
1465	## Evidence
1466	Advertisers will continue to profit from television advertising, in spite of the 10-15% increase in cost.
1467	## Evaluate
1468	On Strengthen questions, we often start by thinking of possible objections so that we can think about the vulnerabilities of the argument.
1469	Given that... advertisers will still profit from TV advertising, How could one argue that... advertising time **will** be harder to sell next fall (when it costs 10-15% more)?
1470	The fact that a business still profits from X doesn't mean that X is just as attractive a prospect as it was before. After all, say that I used to make $300 of profit a day making fruit smoothies for people, but then the cost of my supplies goes up and I can only make $200 of profit a day. Yes, I'm still profiting, but the smoothie business might not seem so appealing anymore. I might be tempted to try a different business venture.
1471	Similarly, advertisers might see that their profits from TV advertising have shrunk now that it's more expensive, and thus decide to spend their ad dollars elsewhere. Maybe they think, "Sure, I can still profit from TV advertising... but I could profit **even more** by advertising in magazines or on the internet."
1472	## Goal
1473	Look for an answer that makes alternative options for advertising sound less appealing (thus making TV advertising still an attractive option). Or look for an answer making TV advertising sound more attractive to advertisers than it was before (like, "Sure the commercials will cost a little more to buy, but the audience is bigger / people have more money to spend right now / we can better target customers for each product").
1474	--- CHOICE NOTES
1475	(A) This is telling us that in addition to the fact that ad time will be more expensive, the production costs of the actual products have also gone up.
1476	
1477	If a company's costs to make its product have gone up 3 to 7%, then their profit margin has already shrunk. Now if they also have to pay 10-15% more for ads, they might be down to a crazy-small profit margin.
1478	
1479	To put it another way, the ads are more expensive than before. If you had extra money in your bank account, you probably wouldn't mind that you have to pay 10-15% more than before. But if you have less money in your business's bank account (because you have to pay higher costs to produce and distribute your product), then you'll be more turned off by the higher price of TV advertising time.
1480	(B) The fact that the system for rating audience size will change tells us nothing. Will it change for the better? Change for the worse?
1481	
1482	Will it change in a way that makes buying TV advertising time more attractive / less attractive? We have no idea.
1483	(C) This doesn't sound like the positive-sounding change we'd like to hear about. It sounds more like a negative. You're **not** able to do something you used to be able to do.
1484	
1485	In particular, if your business can't afford the higher prices for commercial time, maybe you'd consider switching from a 30 second ad to a 20 second ad, which you can still afford.
1486	
1487	But if the TV stations discontinue 20 second ads, then you don't really have an option to buy an ad anymore. Thus, this answer sounds like a change that would potentially make it harder for some businesses to buy advertising time, and thus a reason why it might be **harder** to sell advertising time.
1488	(D) We don't care whether a commercial is being bought by a provider of a service or a provider of a product.
1489	
1490	For either type of business, the ad time will cost 10-15% more. For either type of business, buying an advertisement will continue to be profitable.
1491	
1492	So this doesn't move the needle in either direction.
1493	(E) This provides an incentive to advertise on TV (an increasingly large audience to see your ads), so it strengthens the idea that it will be at least as easy to sell advertising time next fall.
1494	
1495	Businesses can think, "Well, ad time now costs 10-15% more than before. But the audience increases by 2% every two months, so from last fall to next fall, the audience size will have gone up about 12%. I guess it's worth paying 10-15% more for ad time, given that it's reaching an audience that's 12% bigger than before."

1606	=================== PT146-S3-Q21 | diff 4 | key A | score 19.5 | features: Explain Curious Fact
1607	STIMULUS: Journalist: People whose diets contain a relatively large amount of iron are significantly more likely to develop Parkinson’s disease than are those whose diets contain less of this mineral. Limiting one’s intake of meats, seafood, and other foods rich in iron should thus reduce one’s chances of contracting this disease.
1608	STEM: Which one of the following, if true, most strengthens the journalist’s reasoning?
1609	(A) [65% | Correct] Most people who have a genetic predisposition to Parkinson’s disease have no more iron in their diets than people without the predisposition.
1610	(B) [5% | No Impact] Many of the vegetables regularly consumed by vegetarians who do not contract Parkinson’s disease are as rich in iron as meat and seafood.
1611	(C) [4% | Irrelevant Comparison] Children and adolescents require a much larger amount of iron in their diets than do mature adults.
1612	(D) [23% | Too Weak] The iron in some foods is much less easily absorbed by the body than the iron contained in other foods.
1613	(E) [4% | Irrelevant Distinction] The amounts of iron-rich foods consumed by people starts to decline beginning at age 50.
1614	--- BREAKDOWN
1615	## Conclusion (should thus)
1616	Limiting one's intake of meats, seafood, and other foods rich in iron should reduce one's chance of contracting Parkinson's disease.
1617	## Evidence (people who are X more likely Y)
1618	There's a correlation between diets contain large amount of iron | more likely to get Parkinson's.
1619	## Evaluate
1620	When an author presents a Curious Comparison like a correlation, they will almost always then make some causal assumption about one side causing the other. In this case we can tell the author is assuming that eating diet with lots of iron leads to higher chance of Parkinson's, because her conclusion is acting like avoiding a diet high in iron would help lower chance of Parkinson's. Whenever an author assumes some causal explanation for a curious fact, we react with two different questions: could there be some Alternate Explanation for the curious fact? how plausible is the Author's Explanation?
1621	## Goal
1622	Since this is Strengthen, we want to either rule out a different way to explain why a diet high in iron is correlated with higher Parkinson's risks (i.e. "frequent blows to the head, which is known to increase Parkinson's risks, are not much more likely for people who eat high iron diets than for those who don't") or increase the plausibility that high iron leads to higher Parkinson's risk, or that eating less meat / seafood / iron-rich food would help lower one's Parkinson's risk.
1623	--- CHOICE NOTES
1624	(A) This rules out an alternate explanation. Whenever we're presented with a correlation between X and Y, and the author concludes that X causes Y, we'll come back at him with two classic Alternate Explanations: what if Y causes X (reverse causality), or what if Z is the reason Y and X are correlated (third factor). In this argument, reverse causality would be nonsense, because "more likely to develop" Parkinson's implies that Parkinson's came later. We can't argue that Parkinson's came first and it led to a higher iron diet. But we can still argue that some third factor is causing the higher Parkinson's risk, and it's either causing or correlated with higher iron diet, which is why there appears to be a correlation between Parkinson's and high iron. Having a lot of money could simultaneously cause you to eat caviar and play golf. If we cited a correlation that "people who play golf are more likely than those who don't to eat caviar", we wouldn't want to assume that playing golf is what's causing you to eat caviar. Similarly, a certain genetic profile might lead to both higher risk of developing Parkinson's as well as some form of anemia (iron-deprivation). That sort of person would both have to eat a high iron diet and be at high risk of developing Parkinson's. Thus, there would be a correlation between high iron and higher rate of Parkinson's, even though neither one is causing the other. When a Strengthen answer is ruling out an alternate cause, it's "controlling" for some other variable, so we frequently hear the language "were no more likely to" or something equalizing like that. If people with a genetic bent towards Parkinson's are no more likely to have a high iron diet, then it rules out the possibility that high iron is just a coincidental factor that's "riding on the coattails" of the real causal factor ... genetic predisposition.
1625	(B) When authors list examples, as this one did by citing 'meat, seafood, and other foods rich in iron", the named examples are not important at all. But LSAC likes to write trap answers trying to get people to care. The logical force of meat / seafood / veggies consumed by vegetarians is all the same: high iron. Being told that some veggies are also high in iron means nothing. It doesn't help us investigate whether these high iron diets (whether that iron is coming from meat / fish / veggies) is the causal factor creating a higher risk of Parkinson's.
1626	(C) The fact that kids are more likely to eat iron than adults doesn't have any clear impact on whether high iron has a causal effect on Parkinson's risk. We're trying to compare people at the same stage of life in terms of more iron / less iron in their diet. The author is thinking, given two 30 year old people (or two 40 year old's, or two 10 year old's), the person limiting their intake of iron-rich foods will be less likely to develop Parkinson's.
1627	(D) This is such a weak idea. When you woke up today, did you think that "Every single food in the world was identical in terms of how quickly the body can absorb its iron content"? Of course not. So this answer is only telling you what you already knew from common sense: differences and fluctuations exist.
1628	(E) This is similar to (C) in the sense that we aren't interested in comparing one age group to another, since the argument never cared about that. We're only interested in comparing people of any given age group with an iron-rich diet to people of that same age group with a less iron-rich diet, in relation to whether or not they have or ultimately develop Parkinson's.

The author is thinking that warmer winters will bring less snow (it won't be cold enough for snow, so the precipitation will fall as rain). Hence, the snowpack will be smaller. Maybe in previous years, the snowpack was 10 ft deep, and now it'll be like 6 feet deep. Why will it melt earlier? Well, if the winter temperatures are warmer then it's probably the case that we reach the thawing / melting temperatures of spring earlier, too. Why will it melt more rapidly? This is probably just referring to 6 ft vs. 10 ft, the idea that once temperatures rise enough to start the thawing / melting process, it will take less time to melt 6 ft than it will 10 ft. (We might also plug in outside knowledge that the thicker the snowpack is, the more it self insulates, kind of like the way the deeper water in a swimming pool will stay colder than the shallow water) Why will there be more spring flooding? Well, floods come from a rapid increase of water, so the author must be thinking that because the snow will melt more rapidly, there will be more floods. Why will there be less storable water? If there's only 6 ft of snow vs. 10 ft., then that's less water. I guess I'm confused though why the extra rain doesn't make up for it. I don't really understand where storable water comes from (is it gathered more easily from melting snow than from rain?) Yeesh, what an exhausting argument to try to unpack and understand. If we were trying to think of potential objections, we'd pull out our Comparison + Prediction toolbox and think, "What else might change in this future world that could actually compensate and either add more snow than the author is thinking or slow down the melting process?"

803	## Conclusion (therefore)
804	Sticking with her current platform will allow Whalley to win the election.
805	## Evidence
806	If Whalley sticks with her current platform, she'll lose by a few percentage points among under 50 voters, but win by a bigger margin among voters 50 and up.
807	## Evaluate
808	We need to prove that "if Whalley sticks with current platform, she'll win the election", and what we know is that "if she sticks with current platform, she'll win among 50+ voters, while losing by a smaller margin with voters under 50".
809	  So the answer needs to convince us that "winning among 50+ voters, while losing with voters under 50, is a recipe that guarantees a win in the election."

817	  If there were 100 voters under 50 and 100 voters 50 and up, then W would get 105 of the 200 votes, and X would get only 95 votes. So W would win the election.

819	```
820	           vote for W   vote for X
821	50+        60%          40%   (100ppl)
822	18-49      45%          55%   (300ppl)
823	```
824	  In this election, W would get 195 of the 400 votes, and X would get 205 votes, so X would win.

826	The only way that W can lose the election is if there are more younger voters (under 50) than there are older voters.

To refute the conditional "If X, then Y", you need a counterexample with these two qualities - X is true - Y isn't true For example, to refute, "If you're a boy, you like sports", you need a counterexample example with these two qualities - this person is a boy - this person doesn't like sports We're trying to refute, "if it's legitimate literature, then it must represent events or actions occurring in sequence."
